A similar reform to those in BPS and England also took place in Chicago in 2009, albeit in a more abrupt manner. The city abandoned a capped version of the
Boston mechanism used for the allocation of seats at selective high schools in the middle of the allocation process in 2009. A few months after the preferences were
submitted under the capped Boston mechanism but before the placements were announced, officials at Chicago Public Schools (CPS) asked the students to resubmit their preferences under a capped version of DA.
This incident was reported in a
Chicago Sun-Times
story as follows
(
Rossi 2009
)
:

“Poring over data about eighth-graders who applied to the city’s elite
college preps, Chicago Public Schools officials discovered an alarming
pattern.

High-scoring kids were being rejected simply because of the order in
which they listed their college prep preferences.

‘I couldn‘t believe it,’ schools CEO Ron Huberman said. ‘It’s terrible.’

CPS officials said Wednesday they have decided to let any eighth-grader
who applied to a college prep for fall 2010 admission re-rank their preferences
to better conform with a new selection system.”

In addition to the manipulability of the capped version of the Boston mechanism, as highlighted by Huberman’s outrage, its failure to satisfy
no justified envy
was also a major concern in Chicago.
Thus, in a redesign following a reform path similar to those in Boston and England, the Boston mechanism’s failure to meet two critical desiderata led to its replacement in yet another major school district,
along with the adoption of a variant of DA. Remarkably, these failures were deemed so unacceptable that officials could not even wait for the next academic year to correct the mechanism,
instead changing student assignments mid-process.

6.13
Broader Impact in Education Market Design

Balinski and Sönmez 1999
and
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 2003
, later reinforced by school choice reforms in New York City and Boston in the mid-2000s,
marked the beginning of one of the most active and successful subfields of design economics:
education market design
.
66
66

66

See
Pathak 2017

and
Abdulkadiroğlu and Andersson 2023
for excellent overviews of this literature.
Since then, numerous school districts in the U.S. have adopted the DA mechanism,
including Chicago Public Schools, Denver Public Schools, Indianapolis Public Schools, Newark Public Schools, the Recovery School District in New Orleans,
Tulsa Public Schools, and Washington D.C. Public Schools
(
Pathak 2017
;
Abdulkadiroğlu and Andersson 2023
)
.
All these reforms have been shaped by design economists.
To my knowledge, no jurisdiction reported using the DA mechanism in the U.S. or elsewhere prior to the publication of this literature.
67
67

67

See Section
13.2
for
Seattle’s unusual experience with the DA mechanism.
Although the city replaced the Boston mechanism with the DA for its public schools in 1999, after
Balinski and Sönmez 1999
but before
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 2003
was published,
it did not adequately communicate the reform to the public. It was not until 2008 that it became widely known that Seattle had been using the DA mechanism for nearly a decade.
Once this information surfaced, the city reverted to the Boston mechanism within a few years.

The influence of education market design extends well beyond the U.S. By 2024, versions of the DA mechanism have been adopted in at least some jurisdictions for primary education in 17 countries,
secondary education in 21 countries, and higher education in 32 countries
(
Neilson 2024
)
. These countries include Chile, England, France, Hungary, India, Spain, and Sweden

(
Pathak and Sönmez 2013
;
Baswana et al. 2019
;
Fack et al. 2019
;
Andersson et al. 2024
)
.

As apparent from these figures, DA has emerged as the most widely used mechanism for higher education globally over the last two decades, with the Boston mechanism a distant second, being used in 11 countries.
In contrast, for primary and secondary education, the Boston mechanism remains the most common, with 50 and 47 countries relying on it in at least some of their jurisdictions for primary and secondary education,
respectively
(
Neilson 2024
)
.

One possible explanation for why the DA mechanism and its variants have become the clear choice in higher education but not in primary or secondary education lies in
differing attitudes toward the importance of the
no justified envy
axiom in these settings. As noted earlier in this section,
Balinski and Sönmez 1999
and
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 2003

argue that
no justified envy
is more likely to be viewed as indispensable in settings where priorities are determined through centralized exams. If this hypothesis holds,
it could explain why versions of the DA are more prevalent in higher education, where reliance on performance metrics such as centralized exams is more common globally than in primary or secondary education.

As we have emphasized throughout this section, the vulnerability of the Boston mechanism to preference manipulation has been a key factor in its replacement by variants of the
DA mechanism in many jurisdictions. Over the last two decades, numerous jurisdictions have also adopted hybrid mechanisms that combine features of the DA and
Boston mechanisms to reduce vulnerability to preference manipulation. Among these, the most prominent is the
parallel mechanism
, first introduced in China in 2001 in
Hunan province for military academies, later adopted by Jiangsu in 2005 and Zhejiang in 2007, and eventually implemented by 28 out of 31 provinces by 2012
(
Chen and Kesten 2017
)
.
68
68

68

See Section
13.3

for a closely related
Taiwan Deduction
mechanism.

Versions of the Boston mechanism had been used for college assignments in several Chinese provinces since the 1980s, where baseline priorities depend on
scores from centralized exams. As with the parental concerns about the vulnerability of the Boston mechanism to preference manipulation in several U.S. school districts, discussed in Section
6.9
,
Chinese parents also expressed similar worries (
Chen and Kesten 2017
, p. 101):
69
69

69

Chen and Kesten 2017
credit the original source of these parent quotes to
Nie 2007
.

“A good score in the college entrance exam is worth less than a good strategy in the ranking of colleges.”

Another parent lamented:

“My child has been among the best students in his school and school district. He achieved a score of 632 in the college entrance exam last year.
Unfortunately, he was not accepted by his first choice. After his first choice rejected him, his second and third choices were already full. My child had no choice but to repeat his senior year.”

To address these concerns, most Chinese provinces adopted the parallel mechanism, introduced by Zhenyi Wu,
the director of undergraduate admissions at Tsinghua University from 1999 to 2002
(
Chen and Kesten 2017
)
.

Under this mechanism, student preferences are divided into several bands. For example, the first band may contain three schools, the second band five schools, and the third band eight schools.
Given a preference profile, each college
c
prioritizes students first based on the band in which they rank college
c
in their preferences, and then based on their exam scores.
The outcome of the parallel mechanism is obtained by running the individual-proposing Deferred Acceptance algorithm with the submitted preferences and adjusted priorities.

When there is a single band, the parallel mechanism becomes equivalent to the DA mechanism (or a capped version of DA), whereas when each band contains a single school,
it becomes equivalent to the Boston mechanism. In many ways, the Chinese resolution to the Boston mechanism’s vulnerability to preference manipulation is not too different
from that of jurisdictions such as New York City, which adopted capped versions of the DA mechanism rather than its
strategy-proof
unrestricted version.

7
Kidney Exchange

In the mid-2000s, through a series of papers, a team of design economists consisting of Alvin Roth, Utku Ünver, and me laid the theoretical foundation for organized kidney exchange

(
Roth et al. 2004
;
Roth et al. 2005b
;
Roth et al. 2007
)
. Leveraging formal techniques from market design and optimization, kidney exchange rapidly transformed
living donor kidney donation in numerous countries, saving thousands of lives annually within just a few years
(
Purtill 2018
;
Rose 2019
)
.
As an unexpected yet transformative application of market design, these developments played a pivotal role in elevating the field’s practical relevance and visibility during this period.

Recognition for these efforts reached a milestone in 2012 when our co-author, Alvin Roth, was honored with the
Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences
, jointly with Lloyd Shapley.
Notably, kidney exchange was prominently featured in Torsten Persson’s award ceremony speech as a committee member
(
Persson 2012
)
.

But how did a team of economists manage to develop the tools and establish the infrastructure that now consistently touches so many lives?
This section explores the pivotal role of key ideas in minimalist market design, illustrating their profound influence on the success and widespread adoption of organized kidney exchange clearinghouses worldwide.
The policy and practical impacts of kidney exchange and school choice, in turn, later inspired my systematic application of these ideas in subsequent market design efforts
and ultimately led to the conceptualization of minimalist market design, as presented in this monograph.

7.1
Paired Kidney Exchange and List Exchange

In the early 2000s, Ünver and I were both faculty members at Koç University in İstanbul. As discussed earlier in Section
5.6.2
,
our first joint project
Sönmez and Ünver 2005
focused on the
house allocation with existing tenants
model (see Sections
5.4
-
5.6
).
Ünver spent the academic year 2002-2003 at Harvard University, visiting Roth.
During this time, Roth mentioned to Ünver that this model has a direct healthcare application known as “kidney exchange.”

Naturally, discovering the connection between our work and a healthcare application naturally filled us with excitement and curiosity.
We quickly learned that kidney exchange involves two key inventions by transplant surgeons aimed at improving the efficacy of living-donor kidney transplantation:

paired kidney exchange
and
list exchange
. Both inventions involve kidney patients and their willing living donors.
However, due to immunological incompatibilities—either blood-type or tissue-type mismatch—a transplant from a donor to their paired patient may sometimes be infeasible or suboptimal.
To address this barrier to living donation, transplantation experts developed these donor exchange arrangements, which were prominently discussed in a
Consensus Statement on the Live Organ Donor
,
issued in 2000 by representatives of several professional transplantation societies
(
Abecassis et al. 2000
)
.
70
70

70

The
Consensus Statement

aimed to provide practice guidelines for transplant physicians, primary care providers, and healthcare planners.

Paired Kidney Exchange:
This invention involves an exchange of donors between two kidney patients, each of whom is typically incompatible with their own donor but compatible
with the other patient’s donor. It is essentially an in-kind gift exchange between two patient-donor pairs to overcome biological incompatibility.

This invention was originally proposed by a transplant surgeon in
Rapaport 1986
and has been practiced in South Korea since the early 1990s
(
Park et al. 1999
)
.
It gained traction in the U.S. in the early 2000s, though only a handful of paired kidney exchanges had been conducted before our involvement in 2004.
The biggest challenge was the absence of an organized system or even a database to facilitate these exchanges. At the time, methods for discovering paired kidney exchanges
were limited to rudimentary practices, such as discussions between patients and their donors in settings like dialysis rooms.

List Exchange:
This invention, also known as an
indirect exchange
, was introduced in
Ross and Woodle 2000
and involves exchanging a kidney from an incompatible
living donor of a patient for a kidney from the deceased-donor (DD) list. The patient gains priority on the DD list if their incompatible donor instead donates a kidney to the DD list.

Involving only a single patient-donor pair, list exchange is much easier to organize than paired kidney exchange. However, a major ethical concern, as highlighted by
Ross and Woodle 2000
, is its potential detrimental impact on blood-type O patients without living donors, whose options are limited to kidneys from the deceased donor (DD) list.
Patient-donor pairs with a blood-type O donor are much less likely to participate in a list exchange than those with a blood-type O patient.
71
71

71

There are four blood types: A, B, O, and AB. In the absence of other complications, most notably tissue-type incompatibility, a blood-type O kidney can be transplanted to any patient. A blood-type A kidney can be transplanted to patients with blood type A or AB. A blood-type B kidney can be transplanted to patients with blood type B or AB. A blood-type AB kidney can only be transplanted to patients with blood type AB.

This disparity is particularly concerning in the U.S., where blood type O is disproportionately common among certain minority groups.
Therefore, while appealing from a
utilitarian
perspective, list exchange raised significant concerns in terms of
equity
.

Among these two inventions, paired kidney exchange garnered widespread approval in the Consensus Statement of the transplantation community, being deemed “ethically acceptable.”
In contrast, in line with the equity concerns highlighted in
Ross and Woodle 2000
, the Consensus Statement underscored the ethical challenges associated with list exchange
(
Abecassis et al. 2000
)
.

7.2
Relation to
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999

Upon discovering this highly intriguing potential application in healthcare, Ünver and I expressed to Roth our keen interest in initiating a joint research project on kidney exchange.
To our disappointment, Roth initially declined our invitation, citing two reasons. First, he was skeptical that economists could independently write the paper without the involvement of a transplantation expert.
Second, he believed there wasn’t much formal analysis left to undertake in kidney exchange, as this setting was a direct application of
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
.

At first glance, Roth’s reservations seemed justified. The parallel with
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
,
forming the basis of the ”discovery-invention chain” that links innovations in house allocation to kidney exchange, is grounded in the following observations.

As explored in Section
5.6.1
,
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
introduced a generalized
top trading cycles
(TTC) mechanism,
allocating two types of houses to two types of individuals.
Existing tenants, one type of individuals, have the option to retain their current houses if they choose to do so. The other type of individuals, the newcomers, do not have any claim to specific houses.
The two types of houses include the occupied houses earmarked for their existing tenants and the vacant houses which are not tied to any specific individuals.

How does this setting relate to an application in healthcare?

From a modeling perspective, in the context of kidney exchange:

•

Patients with incompatible (or less than ideal) living donors play a role similar to existing tenants who desire to receive more-preferred houses than the houses they currently occupy.

•

Living donors of patients play a role similar to occupied houses by existing tenants.

•

Patients in DD lists who have no living donors play a role similar to newcomers who do not currently occupy any house.

•

DD kidneys or living-donor kidneys from non-directed donors play a role similar to vacant houses.
72
72

72

In
Roth et al. 2004
,
the analogy with vacant houses was only given for DD kidneys. The same analogy was later introduced for non-directed donor kidneys in
Roth et al. 2006
,
which also introduced and advocated for non-simultaneous
donor chains
initiated by non-directed (a.k.a. good Samaritan) donors.

Similar to existing tenants who can retain their current houses in
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
, patients with living donors can retain their donors if they wish to do so.
Just as newcomers have no claim on any specific house, patients on the DD list also have no claim on any specific kidney.
Finally, as in the case where all individuals collectively own all vacant houses in house allocation, all patients have joint claims to all DD kidneys.

Drawing on these analogies, the
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
variant of the TTC mechanism finds a direct application in kidney exchange. By managing claims for “unattached” houses,
which could be either vacant or vacated during the procedure, through an exogenous priority list, the TTC mechanism organizes two distinct types of transactions:
cycles
and
chains
.

In a cycle, existing tenants exchange their occupied houses, with paired kidney exchange corresponding to a cycle involving only two patients with living donors.
In a chain, an individual exchanges their priority for an “unattached” house, and the remaining individuals trade their currently occupied houses.
List exchange corresponds to a chain involving one patient with a living donor and the highest-priority patient on the DD list.

Despite the strong parallels between kidney exchange and house allocation with existing tenant settings, Ünver and I were convinced of the substantial value in pursuing this project.
One key reason for our optimism was the ethical concerns surrounding list exchanges and the potential to modify the
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
variant of the TTC mechanism to address these issues.
While using exogenous priority lists, as in TTC, remained a viable approach for regulating chains in kidney exchange, we recognized that alternative

chain selection rules
could mitigate, or even eliminate, the adverse effects of list exchange on blood-type O patients on the DD list.

We believed this added flexibility would be highly valuable in this context. For example, welfare gains from chains, which represent generalized list exchanges,
could be incorporated into the system without negatively impacting the balance of blood type O patients on the DD list.
By leveraging this insight, both goals of the transplantation community could be addressed through a generalization of the TTC mechanism,
rather than treating kidney exchange simply as a direct application of
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
. In our view, this generalization offered significant practical and theoretical value.

Another reason for our optimism was the potential to simulate the number of transplants that could be achieved through an organized system.
After studying numerous papers on kidney transplantation, we became confident that this project could be undertaken by economists alone.
We shared our thoughts with Roth and suggested that he might join us later, after reviewing the first draft.

Roth gave us his blessing to proceed if we were determined. Ünver and I spent additional time reading on the topic and began addressing the formal and computational aspects of the model.
In terms of the fit between the real-world application and its formulation in theoretical research, kidney exchange posed a far more challenging case than school choice.
Not only did kidney exchange involve far more details and considerations from more distant fields (e.g., transplantation, immunology, and medical ethics),
but there was often no consensus on many aspects of the problem in those respective literatures.
73
73

73

For example, as thoroughly discussed in
Roth et al. 2004
,
the literature was unclear on whether a reduction in HLA mismatch between the patient and donor would increase graft survival or not.

Therefore, from the beginning, it was apparent that there was no single best model for kidney exchange.

Despite these challenges, we finalized the first draft of our paper within a few months.

7.3
First Kidney Exchange Model in
Roth et al. 2004

As a use-inspired theory project, our approach to kidney exchange differed markedly from my earlier approach to school choice.
In school choice, we offered a set of refined policy recommendations (see Section
6.10
).
In contrast, at this stage, our focus in kidney exchange was primarily on providing insights into the virtues of:

1.

Establishing a database for patient-donor pairs, and

2.

Organizing kidney exchanges through a systematic mechanism that enhances both
utilitarianism
and
equity
—objectives highlighted in the transplantation literature.

Our efforts paid off, leading Roth to join our project upon reviewing the first draft. Shortly thereafter, in September 2003, we circulated our analysis in
Roth et al. 2003
,
and a year later, we published a more streamlined version in
Roth et al. 2004
.

Positioned as the next innovation in a “discovery-invention cycle”—building on earlier inventions by medical professionals, in particular paired kidney exchange and list exchange,
and progressing through the foundation laid by
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez 1999
—our paper conveyed a clear message:
The number of transplants facilitated by kidney exchanges can be increased by several orders of magnitude while simultaneously avoiding harm to the pool of blood-type O patients on the DD list.

In essence, we demonstrated that significant improvements can be achieved in both primary objectives emphasized in the preceding kidney exchange literature in medicine:
utilitarianism
and
equity
.
These improvements were obtained by developing advanced versions of earlier kidney exchange inventions from this literature and optimizing their implementation.
Within this minimalist exercise, the primary root causes of the shortcomings in existing kidney exchange programs were twofold: (1) the absence of a comprehensive database containing living donor information,
and (2) the lack of a systematic mechanism to organize these exchanges. A secondary root cause was the limitation of both types of kidney exchanges to their most basic forms,
involving the smallest possible number of patient-donor pairs—two for paired kidney exchange and one for list exchange.
We demonstrated that the effectiveness of a kidney exchange program could be significantly increased by allowing for 3-way or larger direct exchanges or adopting larger donor chains initiated by volunteers of list exchanges.

By addressing these concerns with a range of conceptual and technical innovations in
Roth et al. 2003
;
Roth et al. 2004
,
we provided members of the transplantation community with both a methodology and a class of mechanisms that offer significant improvements in both
utilitarianism
and
equity
—the two
principles emphasized in the Consensus Statement in
Abecassis et al. 2000
. As we would soon find out, the timing of our paper was perfect.

7.4
Initial Phase of Kidney Exchange in New England

After receiving approval from the
United Network for Organ Sharing (UNOS)
Board of Trustees in the fall of 2000, a kidney exchange program—the first of its kind in the U.S.—was established in New England
(UNOS Region 1) in February 2001
(
Delmonico et al. 2004
)
.
74
74

74

To address the nation’s severe organ donation shortage and improve organ matching, the U.S. Congress
passed the
National Organ Transplant Act (NOTA)
in 1984.
The act established the
Organ Procurement and Transplantation Network (OPTN)
to manage a national organ registry, which is operated by a private organization under a federal contract. Since 1986, UNOS
has held this contract and continues to administer the OPTN.

Fourteen transplant centers participated in the program. Despite ethical concerns about list exchanges, this controversial form of kidney exchange was organized alongside the uncontroversial paired kidney exchanges.
The decision was defended in
Delmonico et al. 2004
by a familiar principle:

“This exchange program has a clear utilitarian goal: to have more recipients undergo successful transplantation by expanding the pool of compatible live donors.” (p. 1632)

However, in acknowledgment of the concerns associated with this decision, much of the discussion in
Delmonico et al. 2004

revolved around the precautions taken in the region to mitigate the adverse impact of the program on blood type O patients on the DD list.

In its early stages, the program did not establish a unified database of willing living donors for kidney patients that was accessible to all transplant centers involved.
Consequently, from 2001 to 2004, the program arranged only 5 paired kidney exchanges, providing kidney transplants to a total of 10 patients
(
Roth et al. 2005b
)
.
75
75

75

According to
UNOS database, during the period 2001-2004,
10 transplants were performed in New England through 5 paired kidney exchanges
(
OPTN 2024b
)
. In the same period, the number of living-donor kidney transplants
was 1364 and the number of DD kidney transplants was 1443 in New England. Therefore 0.73
\%
of living donor kidney transplants
and 0.36
\%
of all kidney transplants were obtained through paired kidney exchanges in New England in the period 2001-2004.
These numbers increased by an order of magnitude after the introduction of organized kidney exchange. In the period 2019-2022, 197 transplants were performed in New England through kidney exchanges.
In the same period, the number of living-donor kidney transplants was 1199 and the number of DD kidney transplants was 2381 in New England.
Therefore 16.43
\%
of living-donor kidney transplants and 5.4
\%
of all kidney transplants were obtained through kidney exchanges in New England in the period 2019-2022.

Since it involved only a single patient-donor pair, arranging a list exchange might have been expected to be much easier. However, the eligibility criteria for this more controversial
form of kidney exchange included ensuring that no paired kidney exchange—the preferred option between the two—was feasible with any other patient registered
across all 14 transplant centers in New England.

“The duration that the RTOC [Renal Transplant Oversight Committee] will wait for a live donor exchange pair to come forward from another center has not been regulated,
although the general practice has been to ask such pairs to wait a minimum of one month, in order to avoid flooding the system with ’unnecessary’ list exchanges.
If no such pair is identified, the center can proceed with the live donor list exchange process.”

Delmonico et al. 2004
, p. 1629

As a result, arranging list exchanges also involved operational challenges in New England.
Nonetheless, 17 of these exchanges were carried out in New England between February 2001 and December 2003.

7.5
Forging a Partnership with New England Medical Professionals

Shortly after circulating
Roth et al. 2003
, our team secured a meeting with Dr. Francis Delmonico, the Chief Medical Officer at the New England Organ Bank.
Fortunately for us, this meeting occurred at a time when members of the New England transplantation community were facing challenges with their kidney exchange
program and were open to ideas for improvement. Naturally, members of the transplantation community had less experience organizing a system of exchanges than with the ethical and medical aspects of the resulting transplants.

During the meeting, Delmonico found our policy proposal intriguing but expressed three reservations about our approach.
Addressing his first concern was straightforward. Given the substantial potential increase in the number of transplants offered by our proposal,
Delmonico did not believe it was necessary to include the more controversial list exchanges in the system. However, his second and third reservations were more involved and required additional analysis.

The transplantation community’s Consensus Statement recommended that all four operations in a paired kidney exchange—two for patients and two for donors—be conducted simultaneously
(
Abecassis et al. 2000
)
.
This recommendation aimed to prevent a scenario in which a donor in a pair donates a kidney but faces a compromised situation if the patient in their pair fails to receive a transplant because the donor from the
other pair is unable or unwilling to donate later. Due to the logistical challenges posed by this recommendation, Delmonico noted that, at the time, only 2-way exchanges were feasible in New England.
Furthermore, he opposed the idea of patients expressing strict preferences among compatible kidneys, asserting that the only relevant information about patient preferences should be whether a kidney is compatible.

As far as modeling itself is concerned,
formulating a new kidney exchange model to address Delmonico’s reservations was relatively straightforward. However, the analysis of the resulting model presented a completely different story.
At that time, neither Ünver nor I were familiar with the formal techniques needed to analyze this new model.

This challenge could have posed a serious problem if basic theory had not come to our rescue. Fortunately, just a few months earlier, economists
Anna Bogomolnaia and Hervè Moulin had published a paper on a closely related theoretical model, albeit in a more restrictive setting with an assumption that precluded its direct adoption
for kidney exchange
(
Bogomolnaia and Moulin 2004
)
. If only we could analyze the more general version of the model we needed, we could then address Delmonico’s reservations.
Building on the foundational work of
Gallai 1963
;
Gallai 1964
and
Edmonds 1965
;
Edmonds 1971
in discrete optimization, this is exactly what we did in
Roth et al. 2005b
.

The progress we made with our second kidney exchange model was well-received by Delmonico and his colleagues in the New England transplantation community.
This breakthrough laid the groundwork for establishing the
New England Program for Kidney Exchange (NEPKE)
, founded by our team of three design economists in
collaboration with doctors Francis Delmonico and Susan Saidman
(
Roth et al. 2005a
)
.
Approved by the
Renal Transplant Oversight Committee of New England
in September 2004, NEPKE became the world’s first organized kidney exchange system to utilize formal techniques from market design and optimization.

Starting with the
Alliance for Paired Donation
in 2006, many kidney exchange clearinghouses worldwide followed NEPKE’s example in the subsequent years,
transforming the way living-donor kidney transplants are organized in numerous countries.
76
76

76

See,
for example,
Manlove and O’Malley 2012
;
Anderson et al. 2015
;
Veale et al. 2017
;
Furian et al. 2019
;
Furian et al. 2020
;
Biro et al. 2019
;
Biro et al. 2021
;
Salman et al. 2023
;
Duus Weinreich et al. 2023
;
Yilmaz et al. 2023
;
Yilmaz et al. 2024

for living-donor organ exchange programs and techniques where market design experts took active roles.

In that sense, the establishment of NEPKE stands out as one of the most consequential policy achievements of my career.

This interaction with Delmonico left me with lasting impressions for two additional reasons. From a methodological perspective, it provided firsthand insight into the importance of

custom-made
theory in persuading decision makers and experts in other fields of the value of our contributions. As elaborated in this monograph, my approach of seeking policy impact
through use-inspired theory, closely aligned with practical realities, has become my primary methodology for numerous applications of market design in the years that followed.
In my view, this approach inherently incorporates a natural and effective persuasion strategy within minimalist market design.

The final reason for this lasting impression lies in how this interaction influenced the global evolution of kidney exchange, shaping my future research and policy efforts in living-donor organ exchange.
Currently, kidney exchange programs worldwide predominantly restrict participation to incompatible patient-donor pairs, resulting in substantial global welfare losses.
This restriction poses a particular challenge, especially for blood type O patients with donors of blood types A, B, or AB.
The issue arises because, aside from tissue-type incompatibility, blood type O donors are medically compatible with their co-registered patients.

Our initial formulation of kidney exchange in
Roth et al. 2003
;
Roth et al. 2004
addressed this challenge by defining patient preferences over donors based on
factors such as blood type compatibility, tissue type compatibility, and donor age as strict preferences. Consequently, the paper incorporated a built-in mechanism that enabled compatible pairs to participate in kidney exchange.

In a notable departure from this approach,
Roth et al. 2005b
—developed at the request of Delmonico and fostering our collaboration—assumes that patients are indifferent among all compatible donors.
Unfortunately, this modeling choice resulted in the loss of the intrinsic mechanism present in
Roth et al. 2003
;
Roth et al. 2004
,
which incentivized compatible pairs to participate in kidney exchange. Since NEPKE served as a blueprint for kidney exchange programs subsequently launched in the U.S. and many other countries,
this led to a substantial global welfare loss relative to our innovation’s true potential.

To incentivize blood-type compatible pairs to participate in kidney exchange programs, various policies have been proposed in the academic literature or implemented in the field since then.
Before examining two of these policies in Sections
7.7
and
7.8
, let’s explore how our partnership with New England medical professionals paved the way for a few other breakthroughs in kidney exchange.

7.6
Advocating for 3-way Exchanges and NDD-Initiated Chains

To operationalize NEPKE, it was necessary to establish a web-based data entry system. This system would enable each transplant center to enter their patient and donor information directly.
The process of setting up this system and addressing bureaucratic requirements for NEPKE spanned about a year. During this period, Ünver developed the software for NEPKE using a priority
matching algorithm formulated in
Roth et al. 2005b
.
77
77

77

When only 2-way exchanges are feasible, a priority matching also
maximizes the number of patients who receive a transplant
(
Roth et al. 2005b
)
.

Even though Delmonico initially indicated that only 2-way kidney exchanges were feasible in New England, for our research purposes, Ünver also coded priority matching algorithms that allowed for larger size exchanges.

After NEPKE became operational in 2006, kidney exchanges in New England were determined by Ünver’s software for a few years. Every few months,
our team received anonymized patient-donor data from NEPKE, and we ran the software to derive any new kidney exchanges. This was an exciting period for us,
as we were directly impacting and saving lives with our ideas. Hence, upon receiving anonymized data from NEPKE, Ünver and I would immediately inspect it to estimate how many transplants the software would determine.

In one of these instances, the software produced one more transplant than we thought would be feasible when 3-way exchanges were allowed in the system.
This observation came as a big surprise. At that time, our intuition for the maximal-size kidney exchange was derived from
Roth et al. 2005b
,
admittedly in a model that only permitted 2-way exchanges. To address this limitation, we would then utilize some of the technical details in our proofs to estimate the maximum number of
transplants if larger-size exchanges were also allowed. This unexpected discovery revealed that our intuition was evidently incomplete.

Subsequently, we conducted a detailed analysis of the impact of allowing larger-size kidney exchanges on the number of transplants.
Based on this analysis, we discovered that, due to the partial order structure induced by the blood type donation system:

1.

In a sufficiently large patient-donor pool, the maximum number of transplants can be achieved if 3-way and 4-way kidney exchanges are allowed, and

2.

Much of these gains over 2-way exchanges are from 3-way exchanges.
78
78

78

For small pools with 25 patient-donor pairs,
the gain was approximately a 30% increase in transplants from kidney exchanges. In large pools with 100 patient-donor pairs, this increase reduced to around a 20% additional transplants from kidney exchanges.

Given these discoveries, and despite Delmonico’s initial reservations, we managed to convince our New England partners to include 3-way kidney exchanges in the system,
publishing our findings in
Saidman et al. 2006
and
Roth et al. 2007
.
79
79

79

With 3-way exchanges allowed in our system, a priority matching no longer guaranteed the maximization of the number of transplants in the system.
Consequently, we adopted an alternative algorithm that maximized the number of transplants à la
Roth et al. 2007
.

Our collaboration with New England partners also led to another important invention. Before this collaboration, altruistic donors were not a major source of living donor kidneys in the U.S.
According to the UNOS database, only 8 such transplants were conducted in the U.S. before 2000
(
OPTN 2024a
)
.
However, this number was increasing in the early 2000s, offering the possibility to initiate donor chains with an altruistic
non-directed donor
(NDD), rather than a patient-donor pair volunteering for list exchange.
Moreover, since these NDD-chains do not interfere with the DD lists, they were not subject to the same ethical concerns that hindered the utilization of list exchanges in NEPKE.

NDD chains also presented another possibility. As mentioned earlier, in their Consensus Statement,
Abecassis et al. 2000
emphasized that all procedures in kidney exchange
should be performed simultaneously, primarily to prevent a situation where the donor of a pair donates a kidney as part of a donor exchange, but their intended patient cannot receive a
transplant later on due to the other donor backing out or becoming unavailable. However, this concern was significantly reduced with an NDD chain that starts with an altruistic donation.
Since all patients receive a transplant before their paired donor donates to the next patient in the chain, the worst that could happen is the chain remaining shorter than it could be.
80
80

80

Over time,
even this worst-case scenario proved to be rare and of negligible consequence. Using data from 344 non-simultaneous NDD chains implemented by the National Kidney Registry from 2008 through May 2016,
which generated 1,568 transplants, only 20 of these chains were broken due to reneging
(
Cowan et al. 2017
)
. Interestingly, the average size of the broken chains was 4.8, compared to 4.5 for unbroken chains.

Based on these observations, we formulated
non-simultaneous NDD chains
in
Roth et al. 2006
. While this invention was not pursued by NEPKE,
it was later adopted by two other kidney exchange programs: first by the
Alliance for Paired Donation
(APD),
81
81

81

Starting in 2006, our team also established a partnership with APD.
Based on
Roth et al. 2006
;
Roth et al. 2007
, APD included 3-way and 4-way exchanges in their allocation mechanism from its inception and incorporated
non-simultaneous NDD chains shortly after. Not only did Ünver introduce the concept of non-simultaneous NDD chains to Mike Rees, the head of APD,
but the software used by APD from 2006 to 2008—just like the code of NEPKE—was also developed by him.
See
Anderson et al. 2015
for details of our subsequent partnership with APD, later renamed
Alliance for Paired Kidney Donation (APKD)
.

launched in 2006 in the Midwest, and shortly thereafter by the
National Kidney Registry
, launched in 2008.

Due to its logistical advantages, the role of non-simultaneous NDD chains in kidney exchange programs in the U.S. increased considerably over time,
particularly due to the National Kidney Registry’s success in enrolling altruistic non-directed donors into its system
(
Cowan et al. 2017
;
Agarwal et al. 2019
)
.

7.7
Incentivized Kidney Exchange

While our pioneering role in the evolution and widespread adoption of kidney exchange programs worldwide stands as one of our most consequential policy
achievements to date, a key element is still lacking in a vast majority of these programs—one that has the potential to significantly enhance their efficiency.

As we briefly discussed in Section
7.5
, partly due to events that led to the foundation of NEPKE—a program that became a blueprint for the rest of the world—participation in kidney exchange programs
remains largely restricted to patients who are biologically incompatible with their donors. The efficiency loss resulting from this restriction is substantial.
For the case of the U.S.,
Sönmez et al. 2020
estimates that including blood-type compatible pairs in kidney exchange could increase the number of kidney exchange transplants by as much as 160%.

A handful of policies have been proposed and promoted by various groups to encourage the participation of compatible pairs in kidney exchange programs.
One such policy,
incentivized kidney exchange
(IKE), was developed by Ünver and me in the early 2010s, as detailed in
Sönmez and Ünver 2015
and later partially published in
Sönmez et al. 2020
.
However, this policy has yet to be adopted in practice.

IKE refers to a nuanced strategy to encourage blood-type compatible pairs to participate in kidney exchange.
The primary idea involves incentivizing such pairs by offering the patient a priority increase in the deceased-donor (DD) list in the event of another renal failure in the future.
This incentive is valuable, considering that a kidney from a living donor typically lasts approximately 15 to 20 years.

The ethical aspects of this policy have been discussed favorably by members of the Canadian transplantation community
(
Gill et al. 2017
)
.
In principle, IKE could be considered for integration into the ongoing reform of the UNOS DD allocation system
(
Sönmez and Ünver 2023
)
.
However, due to the separate management of living-donor and DD transplants in the U.S., adopting a policy that links the management of these two sources of transplant kidneys would require broader national consensus.

Despite receiving favorable evaluations of its ethical principles within the transplantation community, strictly speaking, IKE falls outside the scope of minimalist market design.
While linking the management of living-donor kidneys and DD kidneys may be a valuable policy for increasing the utilization of willing living donors,
it is not clear that the absence of this link is the primary cause of the lack of participation by compatible pairs in kidney exchange.
In this sense, while IKE offers substantial welfare gains, as an innovation that requires the joint management of multiple separate institutions,
a reform based on IKE lacks the typical persuasion strategy inherent in minimalist market design. An ambitious policy proposal for an aspiring design economist,
this may be one of the reasons why IKE has fallen short of its practical promise so far, despite the significant welfare gains it offers.
82
82

82

While IKE departs from the idealized version of minimalist market design,
it still maintains two of the approach’s three pillars: it identifies the institutional mission, including its ethical considerations, and employs a compelling persuasion strategy.
The only pillar IKE does not fully embrace is the avoidance of non-essential changes, as it integrates the management of living-donor and DD kidneys.
If separating the management of these kidneys compromises the primary objectives of the transplantation community—a plausible yet unexplored hypothesis—IKE may also satisfy the third pillar over time.
In this sense, it is not far from being a minimalist reform. Other policies that involve linking the DD program with living-donor exchange include the

list exchange
by
Ross and Woodle 2000
,
DD chains
by
Roth et al. 2004
, the
voucher program
by
Veale et al. 2017
, and the
unpaired kidney exchange
by
Akbarpour et al. 2024
.

7.8
Global Kidney Exchange

Addressing the root causes of institutional failures contributes to the efficacy of minimalist market design. This approach helps avoid inadvertently disrupting essential aspects of an institution
while enhancing its performance in other dimensions. Incentivized kidney exchange (IKE) falls short of this feature.

An even more critical factor behind the success of minimalist market design is its alignment with the core objectives of decision makers and stakeholders.
These objectives are not only preserved but also fully embraced within IKE. How do policy efforts fare when they disregard this essential feature?

An initiative by Roth and Dr. Mike Rees offers a natural experiment illustrating the importance of this aspect in the effectiveness of economists’ policy work, particularly when conducted as “outsiders” without direct authority.
To expand kidney exchange, Roth and Rees promoted the
Global Kidney Exchange
(GKE) program
(
Rees et al. 2017
;
Rees et al. 2022
)
. The program funds kidney transplants for recipients from low- and middle-income countries (LMICs)
in exchange for living donors who facilitate transplant cycles or NDD-chains in high-income countries (HICs). One of its goals is to incorporate blood-type-compatible pairs from LMICs,
who are unable to afford transplantation, into the kidney exchange pool of HICs.

Since 2015, the Alliance for Paired Kidney Donation (APKD) has implemented GKE
(
Rees et al. 2022
)
, albeit with limited success. Despite strong advocacy by Roth, a Nobel laureate celebrated for his role in kidney exchange,
GKE has resulted in a relatively modest number of transplants. Between January 2015 and February 2022, 17 international patients facing financial barriers to direct transplantation
facilitated kidney transplants for 35 U.S. patients through GKE, totaling 52 kidney transplants
(
Rees et al. 2022
)
.
In contrast, over 6,000 kidney exchange transplants occurred in the U.S. during the same period
(
OPTN 2024a
)
.

The limited success of GKE, comprising only about 0.5% of kidney exchange transplants in the U.S. from January 2015 to February 2022, can be attributed to
strong opposition within the transplantation community.
83
83

83

For a detailed analysis of this opposition, refer to
Ambagtsheer et al. 2020
.

In contrast to our earlier approach in
Roth et al. 2003
;
Roth et al. 2004
;
Roth et al. 2005b
—which led to NEPKE—critics argue that GKE undermines various ethical norms in transplantation.
The persuasion strategies carefully embedded in these initial series of
kidney exchange papers—strategies that, in my view, secured our team of economists a major policy victory during our initial interactions with Delmonico—are either bypassed under GKE or have shown limited effectiveness.
Indeed, Delmonico, the pivotal figure in the initial collaboration between our team of market designers and medical professionals,
has been one of the fiercest opponents of GKE.
84
84

84

Delmonico not only co-authored a comment titled
“Opposition to Irresponsible Global Kidney Exchange” in the
American Journal of Transplantation

(
Delmonico and Ascher 2017
)
but also spearheaded
an ethical objection to GKE through his position as the Executive Director of the
Declaration of Istanbul Custodian Group
(DICG). Refer to the
“Statement of the Declaration of Istanbul Custodian Group Concerning the Ethical Objections to the Proposed Global Kidney Exchange Program”
at
https://www.declarationofistanbul.org/images/stories/resources/policy_documents/DICG%20Statement%20on%20GKEP_Nov28_final.pdf
, last accessed on 11/11/2023.

Opposition to GKE extends beyond the U.S. In 2018, Rees and Roth presented GKE to the National Competent Authorities (NCA) in the European
Union.
85
85

85

According to the Directive 2010/53/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council of 7 July 2010 on standards of quality and safety of human organs intended for transplantation,
all transplant activities in the EU are overseen by the NCA. Available at
https://eur-lex.europa.eu/LexUriServ/LexUriServ.do?uri=OJ:L:2010:207:0014:0029:EN:PDF
, last accessed 12/10/2023.

In their review, EU NCAs expressed the following ethical concerns, deeming it inappropriate for any EU transplant center to participate in Rees and Roth’s proposed GKE scheme:
86
86

86

See the Statement
on Proposed Concept of Global Kidney Exchange by European Union National Competent Authorities on Organ donation and transplantation,
available at
https://health.ec.europa.eu/system/files/2018-05/nca_statement_gke_adopted_en_0.pdf
, last accessed 12/10/2023.

•

GKE has the potential to violate the fundamental principle of non-payment for human organs by providing funding for a kidney transplant procedure to a recipient
from a low and middle-income country (LMIC) in exchange for a donor who would facilitate a chain of transplants in a high-income country (HIC).

•

GKE lacks clear and adequate mechanisms to ensure the protection of the LMIC donor, both during the selection process and in the long-term care of the recipient
after the transplantation procedure, when they return to their home LMIC.

•

GKE poses a risk of coercion to potential donors in LMIC, who might be offered direct or indirect financial gain in exchange for their kidney.

•

GKE may negatively impact the development of local sustainable donation and transplantation programs in LMIC and initiatives to build ethically sound kidney
exchange programs with robust regulatory oversight.

I refrain from taking a position on whether the sharp criticisms of GKE by the EU NCA and other international bodies are justified, as many of these objections fall outside my expertise.
Indeed, GKE has also received widespread endorsements, including from prominent bioethicists such as Peter Singer
(
Minerva et al. 2019
)
and the Italian government,
which recommended GKE to the
World Health Organization

(
Pullen 2018
)
. What is relevant for my purposes is that GKE faced substantial criticism from various organizations,
reducing its effectiveness in achieving the intended welfare gains from kidney exchange. The contrast between the early success of kidney exchange and the sharp opposition to
GKE underscores the importance of adopting a cautious approach—a key feature of minimalist market design—when establishing partnerships with experts in other disciplines and policymakers.

7.9
Kidney Exchange Worldwide: Two Decades On

Unlike my research and policy efforts in school choice, discussed in Section
6
, which took six years to garner serious attention from academics and policymakers,
our work on kidney exchange generated widespread enthusiasm within months.

Attending the 1995 Summer Conference of the Stanford Institute for Theoretical Economics (SITE), just months after receiving my Ph.D., was a pivotal experience that shifted my
research focus from abstract theory to use-inspired theory. Eight years later, in August 2003, I presented our kidney exchange research publicly for the first time at another SITE Summer Conference.
With its unprecedented potential to save lives through design economics, our work immediately received attention—not only from economists but also from computer scientists.

Within a month, we circulated our findings as an NBER Working Paper
Roth et al. 2003
and soon after shared them with Delmonico,
a prominent leader in transplantation and the Chief Medical Officer at the New England Organ Bank. At his request, we refined our model in preparation for a potential collaboration.

Recognizing the importance of this opportunity, my wife Banu (see Section
12.4
) and I made the life-changing decision to relocate from Turkey to the U.S. in August 2004.
This transformative step—requiring immense personal sacrifice from Banu—was driven by our shared dream of translating my research in kidney exchange and school choice into public benefit.
Within a month of our arrival, our collaboration with Delmonico led to a significant milestone: the
Renal Transplant Oversight Committee of New England
approved the establishment of the

New England Program for Kidney Exchange (NEPKE)
in September 2004 (see Section
7.5
).

7.9.1
NSF Advocacy and Resolving Legal Complexities in the U.S.

This was a period when the National Science Foundation (NSF) was becoming increasingly supportive of use-inspired basic research within Pasteur’s Quadrant,
spurred by the publication of
Stokes 1997
. Consequently, our efforts quickly gained strong support from the NSF. Beyond funding our kidney exchange research,
the NSF also highlighted our unique collaboration with members of the transplantation community in a feature story published just one year after the NEPKE agreement was reached
(
NSF 2005
)
:

“This year, more than 60,000 people in the United States will need a kidney transplant. Of that number, roughly 15,000 will receive transplants from either cadavers or living donors.
But for those who aren’t so lucky, the prospects have gotten better thanks to a kidney exchange system developed by Harvard economist Alvin Roth, Tayfun Sonmez of Boston College and Utku Unver of University of Pittsburgh. [
\cdots
]

The system, which was based on an idea for dormitory housing allocations, has led to the establishment of a kidney exchange clearinghouse for patients needing transplants in the New England area.
Applied nationally, the system could shorten the wait time for kidney transplant patients and potentially save thousands of lives.”

To apply kidney exchange at a national scale, however, one major legal challenge had to be addressed. A subtle aspect of the law’s wording left it uncertain whether kidney exchange was legal.

The 1984 National Organ Transplant Act (NOTA) made it a felony in the U.S. to pay for an organ for transplantation, stating:

“it shall be unlawful for any person to knowingly acquire, receive or otherwise transfer any human organ for valuable consideration for use in human transplantation.”

Since it was unclear whether receiving a kidney in exchange for another constituted “valuable consideration” under this act, Delmonico was hesitant to publicize NEPKE widely,
even though it had already begun performing kidney exchanges. While our partners were not concerned enough to stop performing kidney exchanges, they were cautious enough to avoid advertising them.

This challenge was finally overcome in March 2007, when the Justice Department issued a legal memo stating that kidney exchanges do not constitute “valuable consideration” under the provisions of NOTA,
thereby not violating the act. Later that year, in December 2007, the U.S. Senate passed an amendment to NOTA—the
Charlie W. Norwood Living Organ Donation Act
—explicitly clarifying that kidney exchange is legal in the U.S.
With this breakthrough, the pathway was opened for national kidney exchange.
87
87

87

Before the enactment of the
Charlie W. Norwood Living Organ Donation Act
in the U.S.,
kidney exchange had already been deemed legal in the UK under the provisions of the
Human Tissue Acts
of 2004 (covering England, Northern Ireland, and Wales) and 2006 (for Scotland)
(
Biro et al. 2019
)
.
Since then, with active support from members of the market design community in many instances, similar laws have been enacted in several other countries, enabling various forms of kidney exchange.
Examples include the
Belgian Living Donor Exchange Protocol
in Belgium in 2008
(
Biro et al. 2019
)
, and the
Bioethics Law
in France—initially in 2011 (allowing only two-way exchanges)
and later expanded in 2021 (permitting exchanges and NDD chains of up to three-way)
(
Combe et al. 2022
;
Biró et al. 2024
)
. In addition, as of December 2024,
a draft law for an amendment to the
Transplantation Law
in Germany allowing for kidney exchange between incompatible pairs as well as
NDD chains is pending approval in Parliament
(
Ashlagi et al. 2024
;
Ockenfels et al. 2024
)
.

Following this legal clarification, the number of kidney exchange transplants in the U.S. surged, surpassing 500 annually by the early 2010s.
A major driver of this rapid growth was the widespread adoption of
non-simultaneous NDD chains
, initiated by altruistic donors (see Section
7.6
).

Although this innovation emerged from our NEPKE partnership
(
Roth et al. 2006
)
, it was the
Alliance for Paired Donation (APD)
and the
National Kidney Registry (NKR)

that ultimately implemented and scaled it
(
Rees et al. 2009
)
. By the early 2010s, this kidney exchange modality had become a cornerstone of the
discovery-invention cycle
—a series of life-saving innovations that
transformed our early theoretical work in on-campus allocation into tangible, real-world healthcare advancements.

The NKR, in particular, established itself as the highest-volume kidney exchange platform in the U.S., driven largely by its effective use of non-simultaneous NDD chains
(
Agarwal et al. 2019
)
.
Between 2008 and May 2016, 89.7% of the 1,748 kidney exchange transplants facilitated by NKR—1,568 in total—were achieved through these chains
(
Cowan et al. 2017
)
.

During this period, the National Science Foundation (NSF) continued to support our kidney exchange initiatives, recognizing them as a prime example of how Social, Behavioral, and Economic Sciences (SBE)
research can deliver immediate public benefits. Our work received significant recognition from NSF officials, who highlighted it on numerous occasions.

In an
NSF Science Nation
article, Nancy Lutz, Program Director in the NSF’s Social and Economic Sciences Division, remarked
(
NSF 2012
)
:

“it’s especially rewarding to see such a clear and immediate benefit to the public. This research
moved from abstract, academic theory to real world, direct impact very quickly.”

Even more notably, in a June 2011 Congressional hearing on SBE research funding, Myron Gutmann, Assistant Director of the NSF-SBE Directorate,
emphasized the measurable gains of NSF-supported kidney exchange research for American taxpayers (
U.S. Government 2011
, p. 16):

“ 3.1 SBE research has resulted in measurable gains for the U.S. taxpayer

Matching markets and kidney transplants.
Researchers in economics at Harvard
University, the University of Pittsburgh, and Boston College have applied economic
matching theory to develop a system that dramatically improves the ability
of doctors to find compatible kidneys for patients on transplant lists. Organ donation
is an example of an exchange that relies on mutual convergence of need. In this
case, a donor and a recipient. This system allows matches to take place in a string
of exchanges, shortening the waiting time and, in the case of organ transplants, potentially
saving thousands of lives.”

Two decades later, the potential highlighted by Gutmann has indeed materialized, establishing kidney exchange as a mainstream modality in kidney transplantation not only in the U.S. but also in many other countries.

7.9.2
The Global Rise of Kidney Exchange Programs Since 2008

After the first kidney exchange performed in the U.S. in Rhode Island in 2000, progress in kidney exchange was initially slow.
Between 2000 and 2003—prior to our engagement with the transplantation community—only 31 kidney exchange transplants were performed nationwide over four years. In sharp contrast,
4,387 transplants were conducted between 2020 and 2023
(
OPTN 2024a
)
. A record 1,283 kidney exchange transplants were performed in 2023 alone, and by December 2024,
the cumulative total exceeded 13,000 patients in the U.S. (For annual transplant numbers from 2000 to 2023, see Figure
7
.)

According to estimates by
Teltser 2019
, 64% of these transplants—approximately 8,300—represent new transplants that would not have occurred without kidney exchange.
Furthermore,
Teltser 2019
highlights that kidney exchange has also improved average graft survival rates, despite the fact that kidney exchange recipients often come from more disadvantaged groups.
These include highly sensitized patients with antibodies against large portions of the population, older patients, and recipients of kidneys from older donors.

Over the past two decades, kidney exchange programs have expanded beyond the U.S., providing life-saving transplants to patients worldwide.
Countries such as the U.K., Turkey, Canada, India, Saudi Arabia, the Netherlands, and Australia now perform some of the highest kidney exchange volumes outside the U.S.
Altogether, the number of kidney exchange transplants carried out globally to date exceeds 20,000.

The U.K. was the first country outside the U.S. to adopt market design tools for kidney exchange, including the implementation of optimal matching algorithms,
and it now operates the largest national system. Since 2007, the
National Health Service Blood and Transplant (NHSBT)
has coordinated kidney exchanges through the
UK Living Donor Kidney Sharing Scheme
.
This program, which runs every three months, has collaborated with market design researcher David Manlove since 2008 and celebrated its 2000th kidney exchange transplant in September 2024.

Initially limited to 2-way and 3-way cycles
(
Biró et al. 2009
)
, the U.K. scheme has also been organizing non-directed donor (NDD) chains of up to three recipients since 2012
(
Manlove and O’Malley 2014
;
Ferrari et al. 2014
)
.
Among the 670 kidney exchange transplants conducted between April 2020 and March 2024, 126 (18.8%) were carried out in 2-way cycles, 118 (17.6%) in 3-way cycles, 215 (32.1%) in 2-way NDD chains,
and 211 (31.5%) in 3-way NDD chains
(
NHS Blood and Transplant 2024
)
.

Two key factors behind the U.K.’s success are the substantial participation of altruistic non-directed donors (NDDs) and compatible donor-recipient pairs.
Between April 2015 and March 2024, there were 653 NDDs, with 347 donating directly to the deceased donor list and 306 donating through the kidney sharing scheme
(
NHS Blood and Transplant 2024
)
.
Over this period, the proportion of NDDs participating in kidney exchanges grew from approximately 30% to 35% to 55% to 65%. Additionally, 119 compatible pairs participated in
kidney exchanges during the same period.

Taken together, the U.K.’s centrally managed national system—which incorporates up to 3-way cycles and NDD chains, employs an optimal matching algorithm,
and benefits from significant participation by altruistic donors and compatible pairs—stands out as perhaps the closest large-scale realization of the vision pioneered in
Roth et al. 2004
;
Roth et al. 2005b
;
Roth et al. 2006
;
Roth et al. 2007
.

In Turkey, over 80% of kidney transplants are from living donors, and the country led the world in living kidney donation rates in 2023, exceeding 35 donors per million people
(
IRODaT 2024
)
.
Performing over 3,000 transplants annually, Turkey also ranks third globally in living-donor kidney transplants (LDKTs), behind only India and the U.S.
These factors make Turkey an ideal candidate to implement a national centralized system. Unfortunately, as discussed later in Section
12.4
, my efforts with Ünver in this direction have not succeeded so far.

Nevertheless, kidney exchange is widely practiced in Turkey through several single-hospital programs, with more than 2,000 transplants performed as of 2023.
However, NDD chains are not utilized, as altruistic donation is prohibited by law.

The Turkish Ministry of Health and the Turkish Society of Nephrology have tracked kidney exchange activity nationwide since 2012
(
Turkish Society of Nephrology 2023
)
.
According to their registries, 2,067 kidney exchange transplants were performed between 2012 and 2023, accounting for 6.3% of the country’s total LDKTs.
This relatively modest rate reflects the absence of a national kidney exchange system and the limited availability of kidney exchange in only some hospitals.
However, in hospitals with kidney exchange programs, rates are significantly higher, around 15%.
For example,
Altun and Yavuz 2023
documented 141 kidney exchange transplants at
Istanbul Göztepe Medicalpark Hospital
from July 2011 to June 2021, representing 16.1% of the LDKTs performed at the center during that period.

Kidney exchange activity in Turkey predates 2012, with significant activity reported between 2006 and 2011. According to
Yücetin et al. 2013
, among the 59 hospitals performing LDKTs nationwide,

Antalya Medicalpark Hospital
,
Istanbul Göztepe Medicalpark Hospital
, and
Akdeniz University Medical School Transplantation Center
reported the highest kidney exchange volumes during this period.
Together, they conducted 272 transplants, accounting for 14.4% of the LDKTs carried out at these centers.

Canada’s national
Kidney Paired Donation
program has been managed by
Canadian Blood Services
since 2009. The system operates every four months and incorporates cycles and NDD chains of up to 4-way.
It is among the most successful kidney exchange programs in the world, largely due to the significant number of NDD chains organized through the contributions of altruistic non-directed donors
(
Ferrari et al. 2014
)
.
In May 2023, it reached its 1,000th kidney exchange transplant, 68% of which were facilitated by donations from 200 altruistic non-directed donors.

India, the most populous country in the world, leads globally in LDKTs, performing over 10,000 transplants in 2022
(
IRODaT 2024
)
.
Kidney exchange is widespread and conducted through numerous single-hospital programs across the country.
However, as in Turkey, NDD chains are not practiced because altruistic donation is prohibited by law.
Unlike in many other countries, kidney exchange operations in India are often carried out over several days, enabling the execution of large exchange cycles.

Kute et al. 2024
reports that the
Dr. HL Trivedi Institute of Transplantation Sciences
in Ahmedabad—described as the largest single-center kidney exchange program in Asia—performed 539 kidney exchange
transplants between January 2000 and March 2024. These exchanges included one 10-way and two 6-way exchanges conducted over multiple days and accounted for 10% of the 5,346 LDKTs performed at the center during this time.
Similarly,
Mahatma Gandhi Medical College
in Jaipur, described by
Mangal et al. 2024
as the largest program in Northern India, reported conducting 127 kidney exchange transplants between 2014 and March 2024.

In Saudi Arabia, as in Turkey and many other Asian countries, more than 80% of kidney transplants are from living donors. In 2023, the country ranked second worldwide—after Turkey—in the living kidney donation rate,
with more than 35 donors per million people
(
IRODaT 2024
)
. The
King Faisal Specialist Hospital and Research Centre
celebrated its 500th kidney exchange transplant in October 2024 as part of its single-hospital

Kidney Paired Donation Program
, which has been in operation since 2011.

The Netherlands is the only European country where transplants from living donors are as prevalent as those from deceased donors, with 400–500 transplants annually from each
source since the early 2010s. In 2023, the country ranked fourth worldwide—after Turkey, Saudi Arabia, and Israel—in the living kidney donation rate, with more than 28 donors per million people
(
IRODaT 2024
)
.

With the participation of eight transplant centers and one histocompatibility center, the Netherlands launched its national kidney exchange program in 2004, becoming the first country in
Europe to establish such a system
(
Klerk et al. 2005
)
. The program allows up to 4-way exchanges and facilitated 284 kidney exchange transplants between 2004 and 2016
(
Biro et al. 2019
)
.
Although altruistic donation is legal in the country and NDD chains are performed regionally, they are not managed within the national system.

Ferrari et al. 2014
reports that 82 transplants had been facilitated through regional NDD chains as of April 2014.

National kidney exchange programs were launched in Australia and New Zealand in 2010 and 2011, respectively.
These efforts were merged in 2019 into the
Australian and New Zealand Paired Kidney Exchange (ANZKX)
program. Since their inception, these programs have facilitated more than 400 kidney exchange transplants.

In Europe, similar cross-border collaborations have emerged over the past decade, particularly in countries with relatively few living donors.
One of the most active initiatives is the
Scandiatransplant Kidney Exchange Programme (STEP)
, which involves transplant centers in Denmark, Finland, Norway, and Sweden
(
Weinreich et al. 2023
)
.

Launched in 2019 with the advocacy of design economist Tommy Andersson and initially limited to 2-way exchanges, STEP has been organizing up to 3-way exchanges and
NDD chains since January 2024. As of September 2024, the program has facilitated a total of 82 transplants
(
Scandiatransplant 2024
)
.

Two other cross-border kidney exchange partnerships currently operate in Europe: one between Austria, the Czech Republic, and Israel, and another between Italy, Portugal, and Spain.

Due to the limited size of their national pools, Austria and the Czech Republic—both of which introduced national kidney exchange systems in 2011—merged their programs in 2015.
This collaboration led to the first cross-border kidney exchange in 2016
(
Böhmig et al. 2017
)
and was further expanded to include Israeli centers in 2019.
Between 2011 and 2019, these systems led to 81 transplants, including 33 after 2015 under the unified program, of which 8 were via cross-border exchanges
(
Viklicky et al. 2020
)
.

Similarly, Italy, Spain, and Portugal established their national kidney exchange programs in 2006, 2009, and 2010, respectively. In 2017, the three countries formalized a cooperation protocol
resulting in their first successful cross-border exchange in 2018
(
Valentín et al. 2019
)
.
Unlike other multi-country collaborations, however, each country continues to operate its own independent national system before pursuing cross-border matches.

Since 2008, collaborations among the European market design community, clinicians, and policymakers have driven significant advances in kidney exchange under the guidance of market design experts such as
Tommy Andersson, Peter Biró, David Manlove, Joris van de Klundert, and Ana Viana. A major milestone was the establishment of the
European Network for Collaboration on Kidney Exchange Programs (ENCKEP)
,
supported by the
European Cooperation in Science and Technology (COST)
from September 2016 to March 2021
(
Biro et al. 2019
;
ENCKEP 2024
)
.

ENCKEP led to a follow-on project called
Software for Transnational Kidney Exchange Programmes (KEP-SOFT)
, funded by a COST Innovators Grant from November 2021 to October 2022.
This effort produced
KEPsoft
, a software solution for national and international kidney exchange
(
KEP-SOFT 2024
)
.
88
88

88

KEPsoft
was jointly developed by the
University of Glasgow, INESC TEC (Porto), HUN-REN-KRTK (Budapest), and Óbuda University (Budapest)
(
KEP-SOFT 2024
)
.

Further affirming the growing enthusiasm for kidney exchange in Europe, the
European Commission
launched a 2023 call for proposals for action grants under its
Health Systems and Healthcare Workforce
initiative,
specifically targeting “Facilitating Organ Paired Exchange”
(
European Commission 2023
)
. This initiative aims to harmonize organ donation and transplantation practices across EU member states by developing a
shared algorithm for matching donor-patient pairs and establishing common protocols, platforms, and governance frameworks. The ultimate goal is to create an EU-wide system that expands kidney exchange
opportunities and improves patient outcomes.
89
89

89

The
EURO-KEP
project, led by the
Spanish National Transplant Organization (ONT)
,
was the sole project funded under this call and is supported by
KEPsoft
software
(
KEP-SOFT 2024
)
.

8
U.S. Army’s Branching Process

My holistic approach to research and policy in school choice (see Section
6
) and kidney exchange (see Section
7
)
was primarily driven by instinct. Early in my academic career, I did not consciously follow any specific institutional design framework. In both applications,
my goal was to address the limitations of existing resource allocation systems in ways that could gain acceptance from decision makers vested in preserving them.

Clarity began to emerge as I observed parallels between the economist-driven school choice reform in Boston (see Section
6.10
)
and the natural reforms in England and Chicago (see Section
6.12
). It became evident that the methodology evolving from my
holistic research and policy efforts could serve as the foundation for a promising institutional design framework on a broader scale.

I interpreted these developments in England and Chicago as providing external validity for this emerging methodology.
My approach—uncovering how real-life institutions had diverged from their intended roles and restoring their alignment—resonated with decision makers.
This emphasis on aligning institutions with their original intentions enabled me to achieve policy impact, which may explain my success.

Following this realization, I consistently applied minimalist market design in subsequent applications, starting with the U.S. Army’s branching process for cadets in the early 2010s.

In this section, I outline my decade-long research and policy efforts on the U.S. Army’s branching process, initiated in collaboration with then-U.S. Air Force Colonel Tobias Switzer
(
Sönmez and Switzer 2013
)
.
While these efforts were initially unsuccessful in influencing policy, they ultimately culminated in a major reform of the U.S. Army’s branching processes at the United States Military Academy (USMA)
at West Point and the Reserve Officer Training Corps (ROTC). I led this reform in collaboration with Lieutenant Colonel Kyle Greenberg and Parag Pathak during the fall of 2020
(
Greenberg et al. 2024
)
.
The new design took effect with the Class of 2021 branching assignments at both institutions.

8.1
My Introduction to U.S. Army’s Branching Process

In May 2011, I received an unexpected email from (then) Major Tobias Switzer, a U.S. Air Force officer seeking feedback on his master’s thesis
(
Switzer 2011
)
from the Catholic University of Chile.
Following the basic structure of
Balinski and Sönmez 1999
and adopting the
no justified envy
axiom (see Definition
5
) for his framework,
Switzer examined the branching process of cadets into military specialties at the United States Military Academy (USMA).
90
90

90

Following the terminology introduced in
Balinski and Sönmez 1999
,

Switzer 2011
also refers to the
no justified envy
axiom as
fairness
.

Upon reading his thesis, I found the problem fascinating and his critique of the mechanism used at West Point—henceforth the
USMA-2006 mechanism
—to be valid.
His thesis marked the start of another “discovery-invention cycle.” However, I questioned the practical viability of the alternative mechanism he proposed.

This was a time when I had just learned about the school choice reforms in England and Chicago,
and had begun systematically exploring potential applications of institution redesign with a minimalist mindset.
My skepticism toward Switzer’s proposed mechanism stemmed from its departure from the principles of minimalist market design.

The starting point of
Switzer 2011
aligns well with minimalist market design.
Switzer begins by identifying several key policy objectives for the Army and demonstrates that the USMA-2006 mechanism falls short in achieving some of them.
However, when designing an alternative mechanism, Switzer deviates from the minimalist approach by altering a policy parameter unrelated to the shortcomings of the USMA-2006 mechanism,
as I will discuss further in Section
8.3
.

From a purely academic standpoint, there is nothing wrong with this methodology. In fact, such policy recommendations are common under other institution design paradigms.
However, in my view, this route posed a significant obstacle to the practical relevance of Switzer’s proposal.

Interpreting the proposed mechanism as interfering with an Army policy choice, I made the following point in my email reply:

“If I were to design a mechanism to correct the deficiency, I would have suggested exactly what you did in terms of the cadet preferences,
but I would have kept the two types of positions separate. For the first type of positions, the priority would be based on cadet merit ranking, and for the second type of position,
the priority would be adjusted based on whether the cadet is willing to pay the additional price or not. I think this proposal would have been ‘close’ to the current algorithm,
and it would have still handled the deficiencies that you pointed out. In a way, I felt you adjusted the mechanism a little too much.”

Later, Switzer informed me that his proposal had indeed been dismissed by the relevant Army officers. The failures of the USMA-2006 mechanism identified in
Switzer 2011
,
combined with my alternative proposal—the
Multi-Price Cumulative Offer (MPCO) mechanism
—led to a collaboration that resulted in
Sönmez and Switzer 2013
.
91
91

91

MPCO mechanism
is referred to as
cadet-optimal stable mechanism (COSM)
in
Sönmez and Switzer 2013
.

8.2
BRADSO Incentive Program and the USMA-2006 Mechanism

Switzer’s research strategy in his thesis, which followed the basic structure of
Balinski and Sönmez 1999
, made sense because there are many parallels between the U.S. Army’s
branching process and the Turkish student placement (cf. Sections
6.1
-
6.6
).
The Army’s branching process involves assigning USMA cadets to military specialties—branches—in their senior year.
Branching among the 17 specialties (e.g., Aviation, Infantry, Military Intelligence, etc.) plays an important role in a cadet’s career progression.

Each fall, the Army announces the number of positions available to USMA cadets at each branch. Prior to 2006, the allocation of these positions was implemented with a
simple serial dictatorship
(SSD)
induced by a cadet performance ranking called the
Order of Merit List
(OML). Let’s refer to this mechanism as
SSD-OML
.

Under SSD-OML, cadets submit their preference rankings for branches, and the highest OML-ranked cadet receives their first-choice branch.
The next highest OML-ranked cadet receives their top choice among the remaining positions, and so on. Therefore, cadet claims over positions at Army branches
depended solely on their performance rankings before 2006—a practice that makes sense given the importance of hierarchy in the Army.
Moreover, due to the importance of trust among cadets themselves and between cadets and the USMA leadership, another key advantage of this mechanism was its
strategy-proofness
.
Naturally, a system that allows cadets to be outmaneuvered by others or by the system itself would not foster trust among its participants.

To address historically low retention rates among junior officers, the Army introduced a second pathway for cadets to access their preferred branches, starting with the Class of 2006.
This initiative allowed cadets to receive elevated priority for a portion of positions in any branch they selected, provided they committed to an additional three years of
Active Duty Service Obligation
(ADSO)
